During the period 1949 to 1957 he was Chairman
and member of the Board of Selectmen, Board of
Assessors and Park Commission. He also served
on the Town Water Study Committee during 1969
through 1970 and on the Revaluation Study Com-
mittee in 1965.

Anniversary Committee and servedas its Chairman
until his death.
ORRIN J. FOSS JR.
1923 1975
"Mert" was one of the initial members of the Board
of Appealswhich held its organizational meeting on
November 16, 1960. He served as its Chairman in
1963 and resigned in 1964 due to his election as a
Selectmanon March 7, 1964. In 1966 he was Chair-
man of the Board of Selectmen and served on the
Board until March 4, 1967.
On November 20, 1972 he was appointed by the
Board of Selectmenas Warden of the Polls and served
in that capacity until his death.

SETTLED:
1718

Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It
is about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.

Worcester

Board of Selectmen
ORMAND THOMPSON
SHERWOOD G
RUDOLPH MASELL
Board of Selectmen - left to right: Normand J. Thompson, Chairman Sherwood G. Winchester and Rudolph Masella.
Town government is becoming more complex each year. New laws passed by the General Court coupled with
demands from the State's many regulatory agencies, require constant change in the method by which we run our Town.
Especially difficult are changes which require new expense monies, but contain no method with which to fund them.
With the economy of the State in financial trouble, speculation is for less financial reimbursement and the possibility
of an increased tax rate. Holding the line on local budget spending and capital investment, seems more necessary
now than ever before.
In January of 1975, we were required to have a Building Inspector. This was accomplished by Lunenburg joining
together with the Towns of Shirley and Townsend and forming the Tri-Town Building Inspector Board. This Board con-
sists of one Selectman from each community. Subsequently, Mr. Aust in R. Fitzsimmons was chosen as the Building
Inspector. This joint effort reduced the cost to the three towns as each share an equal amount of the expenses. This
first year the Building Inspector's salary was funded through the Comprehensive Employment & Training Act, and must
now be included in the local budgets of each community. The formation of the Tri-Town Building Inspector Board at
first presented some problems which have since beensmoothedout and is working out quite satisfactorily at thistime.
The Selectmen, acting in the capacity of Licensing Authority, issued many routine licenses and renewals as
required throughout the year. Eight hearings were held this year mostly pertaining to alcoholic beverage licenses.
The Refuse Disposal Planning Committee is representing Lunenburg as a member of the Central Massachusetts
Solid Waste Committee. The formation of the Solid Waste Committee took place late in 1975 and brings together
most communities in Worcester County in a effort to operate a resource recovery system. This Committee offers a
promising solution to our future solid waste problems.
-4-
===PAGE 7===
The Selectmen attended the Annual Meeting of the Massachusetts Selectmen's Association in Boston at which
time the Board on behalf of the Town was presented with First Prize Award for the Town's 1974 Annual Town Report.
It is with pride that we accept this acknowledgement and we wish to commend all who contributed in any way to
this honor.
In 1975 our Town was twice saddened--first by the death of Merton S. Meloon in February and again by the
death of Orrin J. Foss in September. Both of these civic leaders served their Town as Selectmen and on various
appointed Boards. Their untimely passing was a great loss to our Community.

===PAGE 8===
Board of Assessors
On December 31, 1974 the Commissioner of Corporations and Taxation sent a lettertoall "Boards of Assessors
and Executive Officials in all Cities and Towns of the Commonwealth".
It contained a copy of the Town of Sudbury & others vs. Commissioner of Corporations and Taxation & others,
a decision of the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court handed down on December 24, 1974. In effect the Court
unequivocally declared:
.that (1) the commissioner (of corporations and taxation) has the power and the duty to direct
local assessors to take such action as will tend to produce uniformity throughout the Commonwealth
in valuations and assessments; (2) the (state tax) commission has the power and the duty to direct city
and town officers to furnish such returns and statements relative to the amount and value of taxable
property in the city or town as it deems necessary to enable it to determine and establish for each
city and town an equalized valuation which shall be the fair cash value of all property in such city
or town subject to local taxation as of January 1 in each even-numbered year; and (3) the functions
of the commissioner and the commission in these respects are to command and not merely to advise
or educate, and it is the legal duty of the assessors to obey their lawful commands.'
Pursuant to the interlocutory decree to be entered, the Commissioner and Commission are required to make a
report of progress to the Court within six months and periodically thereafter.
As a result of this letter, the local Board of Assessors had to submit a plan to the tax commissioner by April 1,
1975 for bringing property assessments to full cash value and effecting uniformity of assessments among all property
within each class and among all classes. Our plan to the Commissioner consisted of the following information:
1. The Town had been revalued in 1969 and 1970 by the firm of Whipple, Magane and Darcy.
2. The Assessors and staff were thoroughly instructed in the operation and use of the Whipple, Magane and
Darcy evaluation and assessment system. This included fieldwork and record keeping. We were left with the
firm's manuals, guides and forms to maintain the system in equity.
3. All property is viewed on a yearly basis to insure up-to-date detailed drawingson property record cards.
4. All assessing courses offered by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts are attended byall Assessors and clerks
to maintain knowledge of the law.
5. Statistical study of all salessince 1970has been maintained andwill be usedas a base year for established
values.
We have sales broken down to establish the direction of sale prices and the average percentage increase over-
all for a given year plus the same information on specific types of houses. The last information is necessary to deter-
mine value movement within a type house.
A second study was set up consisting of a selection of homes in each specific type and revaluation of these
homes using the new 1974 Department Property Assessment Manual system and the manual figures.
Upon completion of this project the Board of Assessorshave hard data to determine exact percentage increases
required to modify existing assessed valuation of real estate. (It might be brought out that a new valuation at this
time could cost between $50,000 and $60,000. Updating as the Town is doing, by an outside firm could cost over
$19,000.)
Plans for personal property and land were also included in our initial presentation to the State. After several
minor adjustments with the State to our original plan, it was felt that Lunenburg's total valuation averaged 28% less
than full-market value. To arriveat 100% valuation within three years and in order to comply with the Sudbury de-
cision, the Board of Assessors are taking the following actions:
1. On January 1, 1976 increase all residential real estate valuation by 10%.
2. On January 1, 1976 increase all land valuation by 10%.
3. On January 1, 1976 increase all commercial property valuation by 3%.
4. Continue evaluation of all sales to maintain up-to-date market values.
5. On January 1, 1977 and January 1, 1978 make further adjustments as required to update the town's total
valuation in order to comply with the law.
-6-
===PAGE 9===
The present Board of Assessors worked long and hard on a plan that they felt would complywith the State laws
but would not penalize the Town. This action will have the effect of increasing the total valuation of the Town,
however, it will also lower thetax rate if the Town budgets were to remain the same as last year, and the total amount
of money a person would pay for taxes would also remain the same.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that havebeen enacted concerning the
value ofresidential property, owned and occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the payment
of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed certain specified amounts. If a tax-
payer feels that nequalifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and request
an application which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or
a tax of $175, whichever is greater.
Section 22
- Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 or a
tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater, depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section 37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $350, whichever is greater.
Section 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age orover with gross receipts of under $20,000
annually to place the taxeson domicile and one acre of land ona deferred payment plan,
the taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section 42
- Unmarried widows of police officersor firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valua-
tion, or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
- Minor orphans of policeofficers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 in valua-
tion, or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61 A-Section 9 - Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agricul-
tural or horticultural use.
Board of Assessors - left to right: William F. Reed, Chairman John F. Nicoll and Bernard J. Paton.

Park Commission
Numerous projects have been com-
pleted this year enhancing the appearance
of some of our areas. Wallis Parkhad gravel
spread over its playground surface as well as
mowing, trimming and cutting of brush. The
former bandstand was painted and re-elec-
trified.
Our Summer Recreation Program ran
smoothly with our veteran Director, Richard
Stachowicz. Lunenburg's youngsters have
raised a lot of money over the years for Mus-
cular Dystrophy. This year the fair netted
$156.73. We would like to thank all our
summer recreation personnel for a very fine
job: Martin "Bobo" Schlicter, Chuck Win-
neberg, Ken Lehtonen, Kim Craffey, Steph-
anie Markham, Barbara Gervais, Julie Brock-
elman and Ann Blaser. Some of the high-
lights were a trip to Benson's Animal Farm,
picnics and swim outings to Willard Brook
State Park, picnics and hike to Coggshall
Park, Sack Cinema trips, story hours at the
Library, inter-playground bike races, AAU
track meet, Junior track meet (ages 9-15),
superstars contest and afternoon swims at the
Town Beach.
Park Commission - left to right: Chairman Eugene W. Lehtonen,
Supv. Robert A. Garneau, Richard J. Bishop & Armand St. Arnaud.
The Tennis Program was as always quite
successful thanks to John Therrien. The total
attendance these past two summers was 1600 with an enrollment of approximately 270. It should be noted that this
is a fifteen hour per week program. Instruction was extended to Wallis Park this past summer and proved to be most
successful. We are considering an additional time slot at Whalom next summer. Mr. Therrien feelsthat the building
of additional courts is a must. Basic fundamentals were stressed as well as the "fun" aspect of the game.
Our Commission is indebted to our Supervisor, Robert Garneau, and his able assistants, Richard Harper, Andre
Garneau and Mark Tremblay. Without this hard-working, dedicated staff, our buildings and grounds would not be
in such fine shape. The Park Commissioners met with various Town groups this past year in order to keep the com-
munication lines open as well as show our will ingness to cooperate in any way that we can. Meetings have been held
with the Conservation Commission, Council on Aging and interested citizens.
Gerald Beloin, our Town Beach Director, and his staff lived up to their past reputation anddida commendable
job. The attendance was 10,973. Our lifeguards are "Lunenburgites" and are to be commended for their performance
in swim instructionas well as "being on the ball" in case of swimmer rescue. Robert Clarke initiated a swimteam this
past summerand it is hoped that this programwill continue another season. JuneRiddle is in charge of our swim pro-
gram and has the respect of instructors and youngsters alike. Enrollment in all classes was the highest yet totalling
900, broken down as follows: Beginners 235, Advance Beginners 108, Intermediates 55, Swimmers 45, Tiny Tots 402
and Adults 46. In addition to swir instruction, life-saving courses are also available to Lunenburg residents.
This year Ronald Lamothe initiated a pilot touch football program for men and we hope to sponsor this program
again next fall. We assist with a number of other programs within Lunenburg, such as: Junior Basketball, Little
League, Couples Scotch Foursome, Men's Golf League, Pop Warner Football, Open Gym, Teenage Basketball,
Young Men's Basketball, Adult Volleyball and also skating at Marshall Pond.

Townl But he does exist, and I sure would like to straighten

THE KIDS AND PULL OUTA HERE
Yes, Zeke, Lunenburg is a "great little Town", but it
won't stay that way long if your kind of fuzzy thinking gets
Zeke
popular. Perhaps, though, the reason you have such a poor
conception of what will or won't increase taxes in our Town
is that no one has taken the time to explain things to you.
SUBURBIA
I suppose you think that the average new housing de-
velopment will help hold down Lunenburg's taxes. You're
wrong, Zeke. Just to prove how wrong you are, I gathered
some figures on an average Lunenburg development contain-
ing 52 homes with an average assessed value of $20,000, or
market values ranging from $25,000 to $30,000. There are
67 school children in this development - an average of 1.3
GREAT LITTLETOWN WE
school kids per dwelling - which is probably less than the
GOT HERE,CLYDE.. NOW WHAT
WE GOTTA DO is BEEF UP OUR
usual number in a development. In any event, if you multi-
POLICE FORCE + GET SOME
SEWERS, SIDEWALKS LIVE
ply the 67 school kids X $1,246.19, the net cost of school-
A LITTLE BIT YKNOW IMEAN
ing one child for one year, you come up with a total net
Zeke
SUILIVAN
cost of $83,494 for schooling all of the children in the
development for the 1975 school year, against a total of
$47,790 in 1975 taxes paid by the homeowners in that de-
velopment. Doesn't leave much to pay for all other services, does it, Zeke?
I know just what you're going to say, Zeke - "Why don't you bring in a lot of industry, like they have in the
city I moved from? That will surely reduce our taxes." Besides having a short memory - remember all those taxes
in the city? - you're wrong again, Zekel Although industry would increase the tax base in Lunenburg, there is very
little chance that a small amount of industry would reduce Lunenburg's net tax burden, whereas it's a surebet that any
appreciable amount of industry would considerably increase it. I know this argument will be challenged, Zeke, but
all you have to do - and I've done it - is study and compare adjusted-value tax rates of towns, for instance along
Route 128, which have acquired considerable amounts of industry. Based on the present apparent ratio of assessed
value to market value of commercial property in Lunenburg, a $2,000,000 industry would only offset $1.00 on our tax
rate. I cannot conceive of any $2,000,000 industry which in our Town would not bring with it considerably more than
one tax dollar's worth of increased annual expense - i.e., more homes, more schooling, more policing, more street
maintenance, etc. No, Zeke, if your sole purpose in attracting industry is to reduce our net tax burden, forget it.
We all know, Zeke, that growth and change never stop, nor do we want them to. But we must keep in mind that
although uncontrolled growth can increase tax bases, it almost always creates a net tax burden. We must therefore
make sure that Lunenburg's growth and change truly reflect the needs and desires of the majority of its citizens, not,
as is often the case, the needs of special interest groups. There are many growth-guiding techniques which can be
used. Some of the techniques which have controlled Lunenburg's growth and tax rates are Zoning, Subdivision Con-
trol, Conservation of Open Space, and Wetlands Protection. You will find, Zeke, that along with responsible admin-
istration and good financial management, these have been andwill continue to be some of the best tools for improving
the quality of our lives and keeping the small-town character of our Town, while keeping the tax rate within reason.
We sometimes forget, Zeke, that desired changes may have tremendous secondary impact. An example would be
the installation of a public sewer system in a town such as Lunenburg, which with its ample developable area and its
fine school system is already growing at a slightly more than comfortable rate. Besides costing every resident an arm
and a leg, and costing the persons who live in the particular area to be sewered two arms and two legs, it spawns the
risk of runaway growth, as previously unbuildable lots become buildable. Our State legislators are continually faced
with proposals to outlaw large-lot zoning in cities and towns that have sewerage. If any such legislation should be
passed, which might result in the reduction of Lunenburg's lot sizes from an acre to 1/4 that size, our growth would in-
deed be uncontrollable. Therefore, Zeke, this fall when the matter of sewerage comes up, you'd better hot-foot it to
the Town Meeting, and if by any chance the Meeting votes to embark on this project, you'd better run for the hills!
-9-

CEMETERY RULES AND REGULATIONS
AS AMENDED
1. All burials and removals therein shall be conducted under the supervision of the Superintendent.
1A. The Board of Cemetery Commissioners may, by Deed made and executed in such manner and form as it may pre-
scribe, convey to any person, the sole and exclusive right of burial in any lot in the Town Cemeteries, in accord-
ance with the following schedule of prices, provided, however, that the Town shall provide or set aside a suitable
portion as a public burial place for use of inhabitants, free of charge.
Price Per Grave - - $25.00
2. No interments shall be made except in a concrete vault.
2A. No burials shall take place on Sundays or Holidays.
3. Notrees shall be planted and onlysuch shrubs may be plantedas are approved by the Commissioners. The plant-
ing of such shrubs must be done under the supervision of the Superintendent.
4. Charges for burials shall be as follows:
Regular Burials
$ 60.00
Crematory Remains
15.00
(Dec. 1st to Apr. 1st)
100.00
Stillborn & Crematory Remains
25.00
Stillborn
15.00
(from Dec. 1st to Apr. 1st.)
The Commissioners reserve the right to makea charge for snow removal in connection with winter burials, said charge
to be in addition to any burial charge in effect at the time, and to make additional charges in any other case where
it becomes necessary to remove snow or ledge or open frozen ground.
5. Perpetual Care shall consist of the following:
A. Periodic mowing of grass.
B. Raking and removing grass and dead leaves.
C. Filling and reseeding graves.
6. Any individual having a right or rights of burial in any lot shall provide for Perpetual Care ofsuch lot by paying
a sum of money as hereinafter stipulated.
7. The charges for Perpetual Care shall be as follows:
1st Grave - $75.00 and Each Additional Grave - $25.00.
8. All individuals requesting a grave lot, or before exercising a right of burial in a lot shall, before a grave is op-
ened, fillout and sign, in duplicate, an application therefor and submit the same to the Superintendent. Such forms
shall be entitled "Cemetery Application" and shall be in such form as the Cemetery Commissioners deem necessary.
The same may be obtained from the Commissioners or the Superintendent.
9. All money due on a grave lot shall be paid before opening a grave unless satisfactory arrangements have been
made with the Cemetery Commissioners for their payment.
10. In the event of a default in the payment of agreed charges, the Commissioners reserve theright to reassign any
unoccupied grave lot.
11. No monument allowed on less than two grave lot, only marker on one grave lot.
12. All fees due Cemetery Department must be paid before setting of stones.
13. The Commissioners shall have the right to refuse to assign or convey a burial lot to any person not an inhabi-
tant of the Town of Lunenburg.

===PAGE 14===
Library Trustees
Critie Mark
Library Trustees - left to right: Librarian John E. Ramsay, Barbara W. Brown, Chairman Robert S. Tapply, Barbara T.
Murray and John J. Boursy. Absent when picture was taken were Beverly A. Mulholland and Janet M. Cragin.
The generosity of many people has enabled the Library to effectively expand its services to the people of Lun-
enburg over the past year. The Friends of the Library began a series of demonstrations and talks open to the public
free of charge. The topics, presented by local experts, covered such fields as natural foods, house plants, ecology,
furniture restoring and nutrition, along with numerous decorative arts. The "Interest Series" has beensuch a success
that another schedule of topics will beoffered in 1976. Furthermore, the Friends of the Library have shown Saturday
afternoon films, sponsored a children's art festival and have undertaken other entertaining events. They also donated
a beautiful stereo system-equipment that has beensorely lacking until now. The Friends of the Library have indeed
been a great help.
Memorial gifts have also played their part at the Library. The Kenneth Miller family donated a table specially
designed to facilitate the use of the stereo system. This was in memory of their daughter, Kathleen. Three pink
dogwood trees were planted in memory of Katharine Krieger, given by her family through the Lunenburg Memorial
Fund. These gifts are gratefully appreciated and will serve as appropriate memorials for many years to come.
Another source, the Massachusetts Council on the Arts and Humanities, has aided the Library in its role as a
cultural center for the Town and surrounding area. We were fortunate to be awarded a grant in support of the Film
Program which has been in existence for several years now. This money has been a great help in our program of
showing entertaining and interesting films not available elsewhere in this section of the State.
In 1975, books went out of the Library 40, 469times with adult books rising 7% over the previous year. Other
figures include magazines 419, interlibrary loans 97, and non-print materials (records and films) 557. These cir-
culation statistics, taken as a whole, represent an overall increase over last year--a surprising fact since the com-
bination of alarming increases in book prices and a frozen book budget has cut the Library's buying power by about
25%. In these hard times, people are turning to the Library out of necessity. They can no longer afford to buy the
bestsellers they formerly did. The high cost of home repairs and building prices has createda lively demand for such
materials in the Library. Unemployment has also increased the use and need for the Library--recreational reading
for the spare time people suddenly have on their hands and information on retraining for new jobs. Clearly the
Library can perform some vital services for our people, as long as it can continue to provide the necessary materials.
-12-
===PAGE 15===
Planning Board
The major work of the Planning Board in 1975 consisted of the processing of one new subdivision, fifty-two new
lots not requiring subdivision approval, and preliminary work on updating the rules and regulations for subdivisions.
The new subdivision, an industrial subdivision, has a total of eleven new lots--seven of whichare in Leominster.
The access to the subdivision is in Lunenburg on Leominster-Shirley Road opposite Padula's Farm. Approval and sub-
sequent endorsement by the Board followed a review and public hearing as required under Chapter 40A of the General
Laws. The fifty-two new lots not requiring subdivision approval were reviewed and approved as is also required by
Chapter 40A.
A complete update of the present rules and regulations for subdivisions was initiated and as of the time of this
report a preliminary draft of the revisions had been made and approved by the Board. The updating was undertaken
basically to bring the rules and regulations up-to-date with State laws, namely Chapter 41. Following a public
hearing, the Board will act on implementation of the updated rules and regulations.

===PAGE 16===
Housing Authority
The Pearl Brook Apartments continue to exemplify tax money well and wisely spent. The 48 units are occupied,
and the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association continues to be one of the more active groups in Town. Foliage viewing
trips, seaside dinners, pot-luck suppers, Christmas and New Year's parties, woodworking and gardening are popular
activities and the tenants stay forever "young"! They are represented at each meeting of the Authority and a good
rapport exists.
An Open House was held in June to better acquaint the Town with the physical plant at 131 White Street.
Apartments were visitedanda lovely Tea was served by ladies of the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association. The Authority
reminds qualified applicants to investigate the application procedure.
The 10th series of notes for $765,000 were sold in December at an interest rate of 8 3/4%. The high interest
rate is attributable to the State's financial difficulties. As the threat of forfeiture was before US in November and
continues to jeopardize future sales of notes by the State, we are fortunate to have been included in the December
issue of notes which was sold, even though at a high rate of interest.
The Reverend Richard W. Dupee was appointed by the Governor to fill the State appointed seat on the Auth-
ority. Direct communication with the Department of Community Affairs and the Governor's Office is available again
to the Authority.
The members of the Lunenburg Housing Authority are grateful for the continued dedicated services of the Ex-
ecutive Secretary, Mrs. Eileen M. Alleva, and are also grateful for the opportunity to serve the Town in their ca-
pacity.
The Housing Authority consists of the following: Chairman Ann P. Hall, Josephine C. Meloon, Arthur J.
Brockelman, Richard I. White, and Reverend Richard W. Dupee.
Pearl Brook Apartments

Council on Aging
Council on Aging - Seated left to right: Eino A. Liloiva, Beatrix F. Donnelly and Mark G. Thatcher. Standing
left to right: Reverend Richard W. Dupee, Arthur J. Brockelman, Chairman Howard O. Russell, Roger W. Jones
and Alfred C. Gilchrest. Absent when picture was taken were Signe Schultz and Dorothy E. Millington.
The Council on Aging during 1975 continued to learn more and understood more fully the problems of our
elderly people. The Council has tried to findout what the transportation needs of the elderly are, and also in con-
junction with the United Parish is exploring the possibility of services in the field of home care through the Mont-
achusett Home Care Corporation. At present the Town Nursing Service provides a monthly health clinic for the
elderly.
In August, a very successful bus trip was conducted to Kennebunkport, Maine. Also, working together with
the Friendly Seniors and the Lunenburg Rotary Club, a Foliage Trip to New Hampshire and Vermont was enjoyed by
many.
Sociability and a broadening of acquaintances were enhanced through our Third Annual Christmas Luncheon
which this year was held in the High School Cafeteria. Those in attendance were entertained bya group of students
who provided a musical program under the direction of Donald Adams.
The Council has been of assistance in locating persons who might be eligible for Supplemental Security Income,
and is keeping in touch with programs currently being carried on in the Montachusett area in order that the older
folks of our Town may be more effectively served.
The Council on Aging is deeply saddened by the deaths of Susie B. Logan and Philip McNally who served as
dedicated members of the Council for many years.

250th Anniversary Committee
The death of someone is felt by many people, and the passing of Orrin J. Foss is no exception. The loss will
have its impact on the 250th Anniversary Committee for he served as Chairman since the Committee was formed in
1973. Orrin was an ideal Chairman, with his knowledge of the Town and its affairs, and his sincere interest in all
of its people. We know Orrinwould have spearheaded the best 250th Anniversary Celebration everheld by any town.
Wewant to continue his work, and enlist the helpof the whole community to see that this is accomplished in memory
of Orrin's pride in his Town which we all share.
Raymond G. MacCracken has now been chosen as our Chairman, and two new members have been added,
Francis A. Harding, who will serve as Vice-Chairman and Raymond J. Verolini. The other members are Margaret P.
Harley, David L. Porter, William R. Proctor, with Robert J. Ebersoleas Treasurer, and Jean E. Hobbs, Secretary.
An engraved granite marker has been erected on Page Street near the site of the home of our first settler,
Samuel Page. Wooden signs to be mounted on the oldest houses arebeing prepared. Owners of these houses will be
contacted for their approval before any signs are placed. A committee from the Historical Society is actively work-
ing on the history of Lunenburg. These researchers have come up with so much information that it will be difficult
to choose how muchwill finally appear in the bookwhen it is published. Funds for this project were approved at the
last Town Meeting.
The sale of commemorative coins will be undertaken by the Lunenburg High School Alumni Association. A
series of meetings will be heldwith all Town organizations so that other groups can start making theirplans for par-
ticipation in the anniversary year. The 250th Committee meets on a regular basis, with meeting times announced
in advance. All Townspeople are invited to attendany of these sessions and to contribute ideas or assist in any way
to help US reach our goal of an outstanding celebration in 1978.
250th Anniversary Committee: Seated left to right - Jean E. Hobbs, Chairman Raymond G. MacCracken,
and Margaret P. Harley. Standing left to right: Robert J. Ebersole, Raymond J. Verolini, Francis A. Harding
and William R. Proctor. Absent when picture was taken was David L. Porter.

Conservation Commission & Town Forest Committee
Conservation Commission - left to right: Raymond G. Chapdelaine, Harold D. Harley, Nancy I. Sholl, Alfred C.
Gilchrest and Associate Member Jeffrey Dodge. Absent when picture was taken were Chairman Douglas S. Fleming,
Aleck Solovei and Henry A. Anderson.
Preserving land for the enjoyment of our children and grandchildren is of the highest priority. In addition, the
Lunenburg Conservation Commission believes the protection of our watershed is one of the most important tasks our
Town has. During 1975, the Commission assisted by the Town Forest Committee worked toward these ends, and has:
A. Asked for and received under the Self-Help Program, a sum of $25,000 from the Conservation Bond Issue
for the purchase of the Brown property off Pleasant Street. The importance of this greenbelt area cannot be over-
estimated since is it near a proposed 200-house development.
B. Added two pieces of tax-title land: the Albert Oliver lot of 9 acres and the Walter Main lot of 20 acres.
C. Worked with the Department of Natural Resources toward listing unusual sites in Lunenburg for future
protection.
D. Supervised our Naturalist-At-Large tours at which there was an average attendance all year of 30people,
mostly from Lunenburg, but also from Shirley, Leominster and Fitchburg.
E. Continued as the regulatory agency for the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act. The Commission made
13 site inspections and held 5 publicwetlands hearings during the year. The number of inspections more than doubled
over the previous year.
F. Met with the Lunenburg Park Department, Lunenburg Water District, Lunenburg Grange, Lake Shirley
Improvement Corporation, Montachusett Regional Planning Commission and The Nature Conservancy.
Other activities during the year included attending the Annual Meeting of the Massachusetts Association of
Conservation Commissions in Worcester; presenting a conservation slide program at the Library; offering an overnight
bivouac to Bicentennial marchers hiking from Keene, New Hampshire to Lexington, Massachusetts.

First Honorary Member of the Town Forest Committee
Most important, we appreciate deeply the inspiration to all area conservationists of Mrs. William R. Peabody
of Northfield Road. The Board of Selectmen appointed Mrs. Peabody an Honorary Member of the Town Forest Com-
mittee. There were many membership changes this year: Raymond G. Chapdelaine and Henry A. Anderson were ap-
pointed to the vacancies caused by the resignations of Richard A. Brockelmanand Philip E. Pearson; and Armas S. J.
Heino was appointed to the Town Forest Committee. We still miss Rich's elegant prose and pungent observations and
Phil's gentle demeanor which belied a steely determination.
In our society we pride ourselves upon having reached a point where we condemn an individual whose whole
aim in life is to acquire material wealth forhimself--but his vulgarity is only one step removed from that of a society
which takes no thought for anything except increasing the material wealth of the community itself. In his usual ex-
travagant way Thoreay once said "This curious world which we inhabit is more wonderful than it is convenient; more
beautiful than it is useful; it is more to be admired that it is to be used.' If Without some realization that "this curious
world" is at least beautiful as well as useful, conservation is doomed. We must live for something besides making a
living. If we do not permit the earth to produce beauty and joy it will in the end not produce food either.
Town Forest Committee: Harold D. Harley, Chairman Barbara W. Brown and Armas S. J. Heino.

===PAGE 22===
Personnel Committee
The Personnel Committee is carrying out its work under increasingly restrictive circumstances as more laws are
passed on Beacon Hill and in Washington.
Minimum wage laws now apply to municipalities. Union negotiation and arbitration procedures are fixed by
State Law and some employee wages are now determined by law.
Nevertheless, the Personnel Committee, with the patient understanding of many Town employees, has arrived
at solutions which it hopes represent a fair balance between the needs of the taxpayers and those who provide the many
services required by the Town.
The Selectmen and the Moderator in their appointments to the Committee have chosen people with widely
varying backgrounds. This has provided the Committee with broad experience from which to drawon when consider-
ing the many problems in personnel administration which arise during the year.
The Committee has continued its familiar pattern of studying Personnel Plans of other communities, industry,
and business wage and benefit levels, and has conducted reviews with Town Department Heads. Information drawn
from these sources provides the background for reaching decisions for employees of the Town and for revisions to the
Salary Administration Plan.
The following is an excerpt from Section 1 of the Salary Administration Plan: II
Personnel Committee con-
sisting of five members to be appointed by the Selectmen and Town Moderator, and each member shall serve for a
term of three years; provided, however, that of the members originally appointed, one shall serve for a term of one
year, two for a term of two years; and two for a term of three years, as designated by the Selectmen and Moderator.
No person shall serve upon said Committee who is at the same time an employee of the Town, or who holds an elective
town office other than town meeting membership, or who is a member of any other permanent town committee, board
or commission
II
Personnel Committee - left to right: Charles F. Miller, Robert D. Clarke, Secretary Jacqueline A.
Keeler and Robert H. Walker. Absent when picture was taken was Chairman Herman A. Peed.

===PAGE 23===
Building Inspector
The State Building Code and the establishment of a building regulatory system became effective January 1,
1975 in accordance with M.G.L.A. Chapter 802, Acts of 1972. Accordingly, the respective Boards of Selectmen
of the Towns of Lunenburg, Shirley and Townsend held several joint meetings to discuss the feasibility of forming a
Tri-Town Building Inspector Board not only to comply with the State Building Code, but to minimize the expense to
each community. Subsequently on February 27, 1975, I was appointed Tri-Town Building Inspector.
Basically, the State Building Coderequires that the Building Inspectorbe responsible for the examination of all
applications and the issuance of permits as prescribed by the Code for the construction, reconstruction, alteration,
renovation, repair, demolition, removal, signs, use and occupancy ofall buildings includinga Certificate of Inspec-
tion annually to all buildings that accommodate more than twenty persons. The required permit stated above is in
addition to any other permit required by statute, by-law or regulations, such as plumbing and gas permits, sanitary
permits, etc., all of which may or may not be applicable to a respective application.
Ordinary repairs to buildings and structures may be made without application or notice to the building offi-
cial; but such repairs shall not include the cutting away of any wall, partition or portion thereof, the removal or
cutting of any structural beam or bearing support, or the removal or change of any required means of egress, or re-
arrangement of parts of a structure affecting the exitway requirements; nor shall ordinary repairs include addition to,
alteration of, replacement or relocation of any standpipe, water supply, sewer, drainage, drain leader, gas, soil,
waste, vent or similar piping, electric wiring or mechanical or other work affecting public health or general safety.
Applications and information relative to building permits may be obtained at and completed applications de-
livered to the Selectmen's Office, Town Hall. Any questions relative to the State Building Code may be answered
by contacting me any weekday preferably during the hours 8:00 - 10:00 A.M. at the Memorial Building (Legion House)
on Memorial Drive (Telephone: 582-4613).
I
wish to take this opportunity to express my appreciation and thanks to residents and municipal officials for
their cooperation and assistance.
During the period from September 8, 1975 through December 31, 1975, $1,403.00 was received by the Town
for building permits. During 1975, 148 applications for permits were received and acted upon as follows:

Town Nurse Department
n
7
a
a
Nursing Staff: left to right: Jean E. Hobbs, Shirley M. Carpenter, Janet N. Pace and Marie B. Richards.
The Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service is a fully certified Home Health Agency and is a part of the
Lunenburg Board of Health. The Agency offers a comprehensive nursing program to the citizens of Lunenburg includ-
ing School Health Program, Home Care Services and Board of Health nursing follow-up. During 1975a second part-
time nurse was employed bringing the staff to four registered nurses. These are Janet N. Pace, Nursing Supervisor;
Shirley M. Carpenter, Full-Time Nurse; Jean E. Hobbs and Marie B. Richards, Part-Time Nurses. Barbara D. Eley
is employed part-time as the Agency's secretary.
During 1975 a total of 1,365 nursing visits were made, classified as follows: Bedside Nursing, 1083 (723 to
patients over 65); Adult Health Promotion, 130; Visits to School Children, 132; Maternal and Child Health, 20.
As of December 1975, the nursing fee was increased to $10.50 per visit, but no patient is ever refused service be-
cause of inability to pay.
The Nursing Service is certified annually by the Federal Government to care for Medicare and Medicaid
patients. The Agency itself offers Home Health Aides and Medical Social Services. It also has a contract with
Burbank Hospital's Home Health Care Program to provide physical, speech and occupational therapy to support and
complement the Agency's nursing and aide services. There is a very active and enthusiastic Professional Advisory
Committee, Chaired by Dr. Felix Heimberg, which meets periodically to advise and make recommendations concern-
ing the Agency's various programs and services. Annually, a thorough evaluation of the Agency is done to be sure
our goal of high quality nursing care to the people of Lunenburg is being met. The nurses attend frequent continuing
education and in-service programs, so as to keep abreast of changing medical and legislative areas.
Both Well-Oldsterand Well-Baby Clinics areheld in conjunction with Nashoba Associated Boards of Health.
Eight Well-Baby conferences wereheld during 1975with 38 visitsby infants and pre-schoolers. Dr. Benjamin Bleck-
man, a Pediatrician from Lowell, is at each Clinic where health supervision, physical examinations and immuniza-
tions are offered to pre-schoolers. This service is available at no charge to residents of Lunenburg, and is meant to
increase health services to the young of the community.
-22-
===PAGE 25===
Well-Oldster Clinics were started in 1974 and have been broadened in 1975 to offer health supervision for all
the over 60's of Lunenburg. They are held monthly at the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly and a program for the
entire community is held the 4th Tuesday of each month at the United Parish Building. Blood pressure and urine
screening tests are done regularly, with special health-related programs being planned for each month. In October
1975, flu vaccine was made available for the elderly. Information on any of these programs may be obtained by
calling the Nurses' office at 582-6702.
This has been an exciting and challenging year for a nursing staff that was almost totally new to their present
positions. We wish to thank the members of the Board of Health, the Professional Advisory Committee, the State
Public Health Advisors, and most of all, the people of Lunenburg for their support and guidance.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
In 1975 there were 134 Plumbing and Gas Permits
issued as follows: 72 Plumbing Permits for new and remod-
eled dwellings, 7 Plumbing Permits for commercial build-
ings, 40 Gas Permits for new and older dwellings and 15
Gas Permits for commercial buildings. A total of 210 vi-
sitations were made to accomplish the above inspections.
This office presented formal charges before the Mas-
sachusetts State Board of Examiners of Plumbers against an
out-of-town plumber who was charged with violations of
the Massachusetts State Building Code. A hearing was
held in Boston in December of 1975 and the plumber was
ordered to remove the plumbing installations in question
and reinstall them in compliance with the State Plumbing
Code. In seven other instances, corrections of substandard
installations were ordered by this Inspector and appropri-
ate corrections were made in all instances.
Requests for information and guidance were re-
ceived by this Inspector and in keeping with past policy,
assistance was furnished to all persons making inquiries.
Thomas E. Mortis
The Massachusetts Gas Regulatory Board will hold
a hearing on January 23, 1976 in Boston relative to flexible gas connectors for gas applicances. Gas Inspectors have
requested to appear at the hearing to testify on the pros and cons of this type connector. Once a month a seminar is
held at the Worcester Community Center for Plumbing and Gas Inspectors for further review and interpretation of the
State Plumbing Code. The objective of this office is to protect the health and welfare of the people of Lunenburg.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
In accordance with provisions of Section 41, Chap-
ter 98 of the General Laws, all inhabitants or persons hav-
ing usual places of business within the Town using weigh-
ing devices for selling goods, wares, or merchandise, for
public weighing, or for hire or reward, are hereby notified
to bring them in to be retested, adjusted and sealed.
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters, other
commercially used weighing and measuring devices are reg-
ularly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced
spot checks are frequently made. Of the weighing and
measuring devices inspected in 1975, 58 were found to be
correct and sealed, 13 scales and pumps were adjusted,
and one pump and one scale were condemned.
Lunenburg residents have the right to expect that
whatever they buy is measured or weighed accurately--
to assure thisaccuracy is the responsibility of the Inspector
of Weights and Measures.
The Inspector of Weights and Measures extends his
appreciation to various establishments in the Town of Lun-
enburg for the cooperation and assistance rendered.
Wayne A. Balcom

Highway Department
Keep your eyes on that speedometer as you travel
along the highways these days, for to travel beyond the
55-mile per hour limit could help prevent Massachusetts
from getting approval for Federal-aid highway projects
after January 1, 1976. This is the word from the U.S.
Department of Transportati which has amended its regu-
lationon state certification of speed limit enforcement to
list quite precisely just what information each state must
submit to obtain necessary certification for this approval.
The Department of Transportat requirement is that each
state describe its enforcement policies, list the number of
citations issued for speeding each month, and describe the
program for monitoring highway speed. Summed up
in brief, it means that after January 1, 1976, there will
be no certification approval for those Federal-aid projects
which can go a long way toward helping US have better
highways in our state. That is, unless we adhere to the
rules.
The following is an explanation of services per-
formed and projects accomplished by the Highway De-
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
partment on the Town's 76.97 miles of accepted roads.
Chapter 90 Improvement - The County no longer matches State funds under this account, curtailing many of our for-
mer operations. In addition, it is no longer permitted to do any of the maintenance work formerly performed under
the old Chapter 81 account. This is the reason the Town requested the last Town Meeting to establish an account
titled "General Maintenance and Repair of Town Roads" and the Town's share and anticipated County's share of
the old State accounts was transferred to this account, thus permitting US to carry on our work of regular maintenance
of roads. The net result is where formerly we had $72,338 for Chapter 81, Chapter 90 Maintenance and Construction
to spend, including Town, County and State participation, we now receive only $40,966, all State contributed,
which must be spent for construction, reconstruction and any form of new improvement.
Minor drainage was installed in Arbor, Leominster-Shirley, Reservoir, Electric and Lancaster Avenue and guard rail
was installed on Electric Avenue. The Summer Street bridge was repaired and 24,729 gallons of asphalt was used on
some jobson Holman, Arbor, South Row, Northfield, New West Townsend, Cushing, Reservoir, and Leominster-Shirley
Roads at a cost of $13,321.97. A total of 552.85 tons of hot top was laidon Lancaster Avenue at a cost of $9,433.05
and catch basin cleaning cost $2,506.00. The total cost of work done under this account was $44,336.73. Materials
were purchased to continue work on Leominster Road in Fiscal 1976.
General Highway Maintenance - 247.90 tons of patch was used on various roads, with labor and materials costing
$6,619.33. Drainage was repaired or installed in Reservoir, Arbor, South Row, Beal, Northfield, New West Town-
send, Electric and Hillside Drive at a cost of $4,595.92. South Row Road was widened and guard rail installed on
South Row, Chase and Electric Avenue. The corner of Elm Street and Sunnyhill Road was cut back and Hollis Road
was widened. Calcium chloride was applied where needed and road signs and small tools were purchased. Spring
cleaning, sweeping and scraping, roadside mowing and brush cutting were accomplished under this account. Total
cost of this work was $22,999.91.
Road Oil & Tar - The followingstreets were oiled, using 34,112 gallons of asphalt at a cost of $17,903.86 for labor
and materials: John, South Row, Townsend Harbor, Mulpus, Northfield, Eastern, Upland, West, Reservoir, Fish,
New West Townsend, Hunting Hill, Goodrich, Howard, West Street Extension and West Street Terrace.
Snow Removal - A total of $60,049.73 was spent under this account to keep the Town's roads free from snow and
ice.
-26-
===PAGE 29===
Road Machinery Maintenance - The cost of maintenance and repair of the Town's equipment and purchase of gas,
oil, grease, oxygen, acetylene, tires, plow and grader blades and chains was $29,900.00. It is our endeavor to
keep the equipment in the best possible condition to save on breakdowns which prove expensive due to the increase
in cost of labor and materials. The equipment was painted and rust chipped out of sanders, treated with rust pre-
ventatives and painted.
Town Barn Maintenance - Heat, light, telephone, stationery, postage, janitorial supplies and maintenance, office
repairs and equipment and special building maintenance was paid out of this account at a cost of $5,590.98.
Sick Leave, Vacations, Holidays and Bonus - The amount expended under this account was $11,464.87.
Special Accounts - The cost of widening and reconstruction of Howard Street, including the balance of Fiscal 1974
allotment, was $17,340.77. Poison Ivy Spraying and Brush Cutting cost $1,371.18. Guard rail for Reservoir Road
cost $1,975.00. A truck with plow, a truck chassis and wing plow were purchased during the year at a cost of
$27,447.00. Rubbish removal cost $1,127.96. Drainage was repaired or installed in Gibson, Hollis, Pleasant,
Youngs, Cross, West, Northfield and Sunnyhill Road at a cost of $19,145.35. Street signs were fabricated and
erected at a cost of $976.17. Elmwood Road was gravelled at a cost of $2,966.85. Extensive work was done on
Arbor Street at a cost of $9,520.83.
Veterans' Services
During Fiscal Year 1976the budgets for this Department were keptat a minimumbecause all recipients 65 years
old or over, disabled, blind or needy were requested to apply for Supplemental Security Income (SSI) through the
Social Security Administration. Those qualifying were removed from our rolls as SSI will pay their doctor, medical,
hospitalization costs, etc.
Through the process of elimination, a veteran cannot
remain on the rolls if he is qualified to seek gainful em-
ployment. We make an investigation of same and request
a medical report from his family physician. Veterans are
then referred to the Massachusetts State Employment Office
where they are assisted in finding positions.
The caseload for this department decreased consid-
erably, but this being such a flexible department, the
workload can increase over any given month. Transfers
come in from other cities and towns and must be assisted
once it is established that they qualify and are in need.
The fact that unemployment is so high in this areahas been
a factor in our budgets this year. We are authorized to
give supplemental assistance to persons who receive Unem-
ployment Compensation, but are found to bestill in need.
Reimbursements from the State are still runningabout
one year behind. This Department has the pleasure to re-
port that we are amongthe very few who have not hadany
disallowances from their expenditures.
I wish to takethis opportunity to thank all the Town
Officials, clerks and others who have been of assistance
during the past year.
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent

Police Department
Charges of "violence against the person "appeared
far too frequently in the accounting of Lunenburg Police
activity this year and are of particular concern. An un-
CHIEF.
precedented eight "armed robberies" occurred in locally
operated retail businesses during the early evening hours
when they were manned byonlyone employee. There was
0
also one armed robbery in a private home. To date, four
of these robberies have been solved. Coupled with this
and all of the pro-con gun law talk, it is interesting to
note that 748 gun related licenses were issued to 736 in-
dividuals in 1975, broken down as follows: License to
Carry Firearms (5 year license) 226; License to Possess a
Machine Gun (5 year license) 1; Firearms Identification
Cards (lifetime license) 495; Ammunition License Dealer
(1 year license) 8; Firearms Dealers License (1 year li-
cense) 6; Shotgun and Rifle Dealers License (1 year li-
cense) 6; Gunsmith License (1 year license) 6.
The drug problem, in many instances, seems to be
directly related to these crimes of violence. Arrests in
this area are not always accomplished, based in part on
lengthy investigative procedures sometimes beyond the
scope of a small police department, and the problems in
obtaining necessary assistance from overburdened State
and Federal agencies involved in drug related investiga-
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
tions.
In keeping with our policy of stringent enforcement of motor vehicle laws, radar has proven to be a most ef-
fective tool in the enforcement of speeding violations, especially on secondary streets, removing any element of
doubt which formerly arose in estimating speeds. Approximately one-third of all speeding citations issued are the
direct result of violators being clocked by radar. Accident statistics continued to rise in 1975 when 305 accidents
requiring investigation were recorded, one of which resulted in a fatality.
Because of these factors, 1014 complaints were issued by the Fitchburg District Court for various motor vehicle
violations. Included in this report is a breakdown of the complaints, both motor vehicle and crimes against people
and property, which totaled 1301 and which were issued by the Lunenburg Police Department during 1975. Addi-
tionally, we received 5, 963 calls for service, served 545 subpoenas, checked 990 houses while owners were away and
took 35 persons into protective custody, which has replaced the old "Drunk" charge.
It is clearly evident that the workload of our Police Department continues to increase. Again, it is recom-
mended that additional floor space be found so that the Police Department can perform their duties in an orderly and
professional manner. Additionally, it is recommended that considerationbe given to adding at leastone man to the
Department so that under normal circumstances two men would be on duty on all hours during the night time. This
would better insure the safety of both the public and the police officers who are called upon to do the job.
Throughout the year, many ofour officers have attended courses givenby the National Safety Council and the
Bloomberg Police Service. Particular recognition should be givento our Reserve Officers who have attended these
courses at their own expense and on their own time.
The Police Department consists of the following personnel: Police Chief Frederick B. Hobbs, Sergeant Roger A.
Fitzgerald, Sergeant Hector J. Morin, Jr., Patrolman August N. Misner, Patrolman Alphonse J. Baron, Patrolman
Steven G. Whitney, Patrolman Michael A. Bottalico and Patrolman Richard L. Lambert, Jr.

Fire Department
In 1975, the Fire Department answered 313 calls
for assistance as follows: Rescue Wagon 136, Service
Calls 38, Brush Fires 33, Car Fires 27, Mutual Aid 26,
Building Fires 15, Electrical Fires 6, Chimney Fires 14,
Rescue 4, Accidents 4, Cooking Fires. 3, Needless 3,
Town Dump 2, Search 1, False Alarm 1. This was a de-
crease of 13 calls from 1974. Of the 177 fire calls, 109
were alarms. Rescue Wagon calls were up by 13; Brush
Fires were downby 15; Mutual Aidwere upby 11; Chimney
Fires were up by 8; and Service Calls decreased by 26.
Other categories remained about the same.
Thirteen of our seventeen rescue personnel and three
other firefighters have completed their Emergency Medi-
cal Training. State law requires that all rescue personnel
be registered EMT's by July 1977. Re-registration is then
required every two years. This involves a considerable
amount of continual training. This same State law also
requires that the vehicle transporting the sick and injured
meet certain requirements. Our present Rescue Wagon
does not meet these requirements.
Our new truck, ordered in August 1975, is ex-
pected to be delivered in the winter of 1976-77. This
truck should be a big asset to the Town. Our other fire
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
trucks, a 1958 forest fire truck, a 1961 FWD pumper and
our 1966 pumper are in good condition.
We are still in need of additional space on the ground floor of the Fire Station. When the new truck is deliv-
ered, the trucks will be bumper to bumper. We also need space for fire alarm equipment, hose storage, hose drying
and work area.
Our firefighters meet bi-monthly for drills and training sessions to increase their knowledge in firefighting
skills. I would like to thank the Officers and members of the Fire Department for their excellent cooperation and
dedication to myself, to the Department and to the Town.

Tree Warden
Bacillus thuringiensis (Thuricide) is a microbial insecti-
cide that is non-toxic and non-pathogenic to man and warm-
blooded animals, fish and honeybees, but is capable of induc-
ing fatal disease in certain insects, mostly caterpillars. Also,
on the basis of evidence to date, this bacillus is harmless to
parasitic and predatory insects and other forms of life, but is
useful against such insects as cankerworm, fall webworm, gypsy
moth, elmspanworm, linden looper and some control of the tent
caterpillar.
Chlordane is not permitted for use on foliage pests but may
be used against Japanese beetle and other turf insects, carpenter
ants, black vine weevil and other principally boring-type in-
sects. Endosulfan (Thiodan) is labeled to prohibit drift in resi-
dential areas and may be used in nurseries, etc., or applied by
paint brush in residential areas, and isone of the longest lasting
aphicides available. It is also effective against many borers,
i.e. dogwood, rhododenron, lilac and peach, and will control
many leaf defoliators, beetle larvae and eriophyid mites. It is
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
highly toxic. (Above information from Cooperative Extension
Service, University of Massachusetts)
One hundred and forty-five diseased trees were cut down, chipped and buried. The Department of Natural
Resources gave five days of tree removal work, amounting to $500.00. The Town was reimbursed $444.03 by the
County. A total of $8,515.00 was spent on this work under the Dutch Elm Program. Thirty-eight trees were pur-
chased and planted on Leominster Road, Pratt Street, West Acres Drive, Fairview Road and Northfield Road. Work
under the Tree Warden Department cost $5,302.76.
Dog Officer
Dog Officer, August N. Misner, feels that our most perplexing problem last year was the nuisances caused
by the dog population in and about the Center of Town-specifically the disturbances on the grounds of the Elementary
School. Generally, these dogs follow the walking school population causing disturbances before, during and after
school hours, and most recently causing traffic hazards on public ways as well as at the entrance and exit to the school
property. In most cases, the owners of these animals have been most cooperative, but I feel that it will be neces-
sary in the future to order individual dogs restrained permanently if these nuisances and safety hazards continue as in
the past. (By authority of Chapter 140, Section 147 M.G.L.A.)
During 1975, approximately 1,250 calls were received by this office relative to various complaints regarding
animals the most usual call dealt with complaints relative to a neighbor's dog and the most unusual calls dealt
with skunks in cellars and muskrats caught in fishing lines at Lake Whalom. One small deer was struck by a motor
vehicle on Summer Street, ran off into the woods where it was too severely injured to fight off an attack by dogs in
the area. Numerous accounts of animals molesting livestock were reported and the matter subsequently resolved.
We have had a very encouraging year with respect to the licensing of dogs, as records show that approximately
200 licenses were issued through the Town Hall. I wish at this time to thank Mary Padula, Town Clerk, and her
staff for the extra help given this department in the form of a letter program which I hope we can continue thus in-
suring the licensing of all dogs in Lunenburg.
1 have found that in all cases of individual dog problems, follow-up measures are most important and a spirit
of cooperation is found among neighbors involved.

Article 2. To accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, or act anything thereon. VOTED.
Article 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XVof the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administratio Plan of the
Town of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on April 6, 1974 to be effective July 1, 1974, and
substituting therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Committee and appearing on Pages 9 and 10
of the Report of the Finance Committee for Fiscal Year 1976, such action to be effective July 1, 1975, or act anything thereon.
(Disapproved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED.
Article 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of the
Town of Lunenburg "by (a) deleting from Clause (b)of Section 9 "Adjustment Policies the word "Temporary", so as to deny step rate
increases to all personnel whose positions are single-rated; by (b) amending the second paragraph of Section 17 "Paid Vacations" by
deleting in Sentence #4 the words "to retirement", 20 paid days (4 calendar weeks) vacation credits shall be added.", and adding
in place thereof the words "thereafter on each succeeding June 1 to 20 years of continuous service, 20 days (4 calendar weeks)va-
cation credits shall be added.' ", and by adding at the end of this paragraph a new sentence as follows: "5. On the next June 1
succeeding 20 years of continuous service and thereafter on each succeeding June 1 to retirement, 25 days (5 calendar weeks) va-
cation credits shall be added.", in order to bring the wording of this paragraph in line with the provision for five weeks vacation
after 20 years of service which was voted on April 6, 1974; and by (c)adding to the list of observed holidays under Section 18 "Paid
Holidays", holiday, "Martin Luther King Day", or act anything thereon. VOTED.

AVENUE DATED JULY 5, 1927.
Article 8. To see if the Town will vote to insure future compliance with the National Flood Insurance Program by adopting
the following resolve: WHEREAS, certain areas of Lunenburg are subject to periodic flooding causing serious damages to properties
within these areas; and WHEREAS, relief isavailable in the formof Federally subsidized flood insurance authorized by the National
Flood Insurance Act; and WHEREAS, it is the intent of this Town meeting to require the recognition and evaluation of flood hazards
in all official actions relating to land use in the flood plain areashaving special flood hazards; and WHEREAS, this body has the legal
authority to adopt land use and control measures to reduce future flood losses pursuant to the constitution and laws of the Common-
wealth of Massachusetts: NOW, THEREFORE BE IT VOTED, that this Town Meeting hereby: 1. Assures the Federal Insurance Ad-
ministration that it will act upon, as necessary, adequate land use and control measures with effective enforcement provisions con-
sistent with the criteria set forth in Section 1910 of the National Flood Insurance Program Regulations; and 2. Vests the Board of
Selectmen with the responsibility, authority and means to (a) Delineate or assist the Administrator, at his request, in delineating
the limits of the areas having special flood hazards on available local maps of sufficient scale to identify the location of building
sites. (b) Providesuch information as the Administrator may request concerning present uses and occupancy of the flood plain. (c)
Cooperate with federal, state, and local agencies and private firms which undertake to study, survey, map, and identify flood plain
or mudslide areas, and cooperate with neighboring communities with respect to management of adjoining flood plain areas in order
to prevent aggravation of existing hazards. (d) Submit on the anniversary date of the community's initial eligibility an annual report
to the Administrator on the progress made during the past year within the community in the developmentand implementation of flood
plain management measures. 3. Appoints a Town Agent to maintain for public inspection and to furnish upon request a record of
elevations (in relation to mean sea level) of the lowest floor (including basement) of all new or substantially improved structures
located in the special flood hazard areas, If the lowest floor isbelow grade on one or more sides, the elevation of the floor imme-
diately above must also be recorded 4. Agrees to take such other official action as may be reasonably necessary to carry out the
objectives of the program, or act anything thereon. VOTED TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 8.
Article 9. To see if the Town will vote to insure future compliance with the National Flood Insurance Program by adopting
the following resolve: WHEREAS, the Town of Lunenburg has adopted and is enforcing the Commonwealth of Massachusetts State
Building Code, and WHEREAS, Lunenburg the aforesaid prohibits any person, firm or corporation from erecting, constructing, en-
larging, altering, repairing, improving, moving or demolishing anybuilding, or structure without first obtaininga separate building
permit for each building or structure from the appointed Town Agent, and WHEREAS, the Town Agent must examine all plans and
specifications for the proposed construction when application is made to him for a building permit: NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RE-
SOLVED BY THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG follows: 1. That the Town Agent shall review all building permit applications for new
construction or substantial improvements to determine whether proposed building sites will be reasonably safe from flooding. If a
proposed building site is in a location that has a flood hazard, any proposed new construction or substantial improvement (including
prefabricated and mobile homes) must (i) be designed (or modified) and anchored to prevent flotation, collapse, or lateral movement
of the structure, and (ii) use construction materials and of equipment that are resistant to flood damage, and (iii) use construct
methods and practices that will minimize flood damage; and 2. That the Town Agent shall review subdivision proposals and other pro-
posed new developments to assure that (i)all such proposals consistent with the need to minimize flood damage, (ii)all publicutil-
-42-
===PAGE 45===
ities and facilities, such as sewer, gas, electrical, and water systems arelocated, elevated, and constructed to minimize or eliminate
flood damage, and (iii) adequate drainage is provided so as to reduce exposure to flood hazards; and 3. That the Town Agent shall
require new or replacement water supply systems and/or sanitary sewage systems to be designed to minimize or eliminate infiltration
of flood waters into the systems and discharges from the systems into flood waters, and require on-site waste disposal systems to be
located so as to avoid impairment of them or contamination from them during flooding, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANI-
MOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 9.
Article 10. To see if the Town will vote to hear a report by the Sewerage Advisory Committee, or act anything thereon.
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO ACCEPT REPORT AS READ.
Article 11. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Board of Selectmen to file an application with the Environmental

as maybecome available and/or together with the funds the Town Meeting may appropriate towards the construction of a sewer system
and appurtenant works, or act anything thereon. VOTED.
Article 12. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Board of Selectmen to file an application with the Commonwealth
of Massachusetts to apply for such State Grants as are or may become available and to accept such Grants; the Grants to be used

the construction of a sewer system and appurtenant works, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 13. Tosee if the Town will vote to hear report by the Functions Study Committee, or anything thereon. VOTED.
Article 14. To see if the Townwill vote to created three (3) member Boardof Health to be elected by the voters of the Town,
and to elect in 1976 one member to serve for three (3) years, one member to serve for two (2) years, and one member to serve for
one (1) year, and to thereafter elect one member each year fora term of three (3) years, and to fix the compensation of each member
so elected at $100.00 per year, or act anything thereon. VOTED THE PROPOSAL TO CREATE A BOARD OF HEALTH WHOSE
MEMBERS SHALL BE ELECTED BYTHE VOTERS OF THE TOWN BE FIRST REFERRED TO THEFUNCTIONS STUDY COMMITTEE FOR
STUDY AND EVALUATION WITH PARTICULAR REFERENCETO WHETHER SUCH PROPOSED ELECTED BOARD OF HEALTH COULD
BETTERSERVE THETOWN THAN THE PRESENT BOARD OF HEALTH, AND WHETHERSUCH A NEWLY ELECTED BOARD OF HEALTH
WOULD BE MORE COSTLY TO MAINTAIN, SAID FUNCTIONS STUDY COMMITTEE TO REPORT TO THE VOTERS AT THE NEXT
ANNUAL TOWN MEETING.
Article 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by inserting in place of Section 2, Article 1, the following new
Section 2, Article I: "The Annual Town Meeting and Election shall be heldon the first Saturday of April of each year in the Junior-
Senior High School, the Town Meeting to commence at 1:30 P.M. and all required reports shall be made at that time.", oract any-
thing thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLYTO AMEND THETOWN BY-LAWS BY INSERTING IN PLACE OF SECTION 2, ARTICLE I,
A NEW SECTION 2, ARTICLE IAS FOLLOWS: "THE ANNUALTOWN MEETING AND ELECTION SHALL BE HELD ON THE FIRST
SATURDAY OF APRIL OF EACH YEAR IN THE JUNIOR-SENIOR HIGH SCHOOL, THE TOWN MEETING TO COMMENCE AT
1:00 P.M. AND ALL REQUIRED REPORTS SHALL BE MADE AT THAT TIME."
Article 16. Tosee if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by adding under Article II the following new section to known
as Section 11: "The Board of Selectmen and/or the Tri-Town Building Inspector Board so-called, shall issue such rulesand regulations

Article 17. To see if the Townwill vote to amend its By-laws by deleting Section 10of Article II and inserting in place thereof
the following new Section 10 of Article II: "All Town Boards or Officershaving in their charge and control personal property owned
by the Town but which in the judgment of said Boards or Officers has become obsolete, and does not have a value in excess of
$500.00, are hereby authorized, with the written approval of the Board of Selectmen, to sell and convey the same in behalf of the
Town upon such terms as said Boards or Officers may deem reasonable, provided, however, that a description of any such property
sold, together with the name of the person to whom sold and the consideration paid therefor, shall be included in the Annual Report
of the responsible Board or Officer. The sale of any Town property described above which has a value of $500.00 or more requires
authorization by the Town Meeting." or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO AMEND THE TOWN BY-LAWS BY
DELETING SECTION10 OF ARTICLE Il AND INSERTING IN PLACETHEREOF THEFOLLOWING NEW SECTION 10 OF ARTICLE II;
"ALL TOWN BOARDS OR OFFICERS HAVING IN THEIR CHARGE OR CONTROL PERSONAL PROPERTY OWNED BY THE TOWN
WHICH, IN THE BEST JUDGMENT OF SAID BOARDS OR OFFICERS HAS BECOME OBSOLETE AND DOES NOT HAVE A VALUE
IN EXCESS OF $500.00 AS REASONABLY ESTIMATED BYTHEM, ARE HEREBY AUTHORIZED, WITH THE PRIORWRITTEN APPROV-
AL OF THE BOARD OF SELECTMEN, TO SELLAND CONVEYTHE SAME IN BEHALF OF THETOWN UPON SUCH TERMS AS SAID
BOARDS OR OFFICERS DEEM REASONABLE, PROVIDED, HOWEVER, THAT A DESCRIPTION OF SUCH PROPERTY SOLD, TO-
GETHER WITH THE NAME AND ADDRESS OF THE BUYER AND THE CONSIDERATIO PAID THEREFOR, SHALL BE INCLUDED IN
THE ANNUAL REPORT OF THE RESPONSIBLE BOARD OR OFFICER FOR THE YEAR IN WHICH THE PROPERTY WAS SOLD. NO
SUCH PERSONAL PROPERTY, THE ESTIMATED VALUE OF WHICH EXCEEDS $500.00, SHALL BE SOLD WITHOUT PRIOR AUTHO-
RIZATION BY A TOWN MEETING.

Article 20. To see if the Town will vote to establish a Water Study Committee consisting of seven members to be chosen by
the Board of Selectmen, provided, however, that three shall be voters-at-large of Lunenburg and four shall be members of the Dev-
elopment and Industrial Commission, Lunenburg Planning Board, Board of Water Commissioners of the Lunenburg Water District and
Lunenburg Conservation Commission, respectively, any vacancies which mayoccurto be filled by the Board of Selectmen, said Com-
mittee to study the present and future water needs of the Town; develop a master planwith timetables and cost estimates where pos-
sible, including in said plan, but not limited to, future and domestic and industrial water needs, ecological and environmental con-
siderations, and the advisability of the Town assuming the responsibility for water supply and distribution, said Committee to report
to the Town not later than thenext Annual Town Meeting, said report to contain the Committee's findings with specific recommenda-
tions regarding water needs over the next five year period and general recommendations concerning the water needs for the five year
period thereafter; the Board of Selectmen to have the right in its discretion to extend the life of the Committee but not beyond the
Annual Town Meeting of 1978, or act anything thereon. VOTED THAT THE TOWN ESTABLISH A WATER STUDY COMMITTEE TO
STUDY THE PRESENT AND FUTURE DOMESTIC AND INDUSTRIAL WATER NEEDS OF THE TOWN, SUCH COMMITTEE TO CON-
SIST OF FIVE MEMBERS, ONE VOTER AT LARGE WHO RESIDES IN THE WATER DISTRICT AND ONE VOTER AT LARGE WHO
RESIDES OUTSIDE THE WATER DISTRICT TO BE APPOINTED BY THE SELECTMEN AND MODERATOR, AND THREE ADDITIONAL
MEMBERS, ONE TO BE APPOINTED THE DEVELOPMENT AND INDUSTRIAL COMMISSION, ONE TO APPOINTED BY THE
PLANNING BOARD AND ONE TO BEAPPOINTED BYTHE BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS OF THE LUNENBURG WATER DISTRICT
FROM THEIR RESPECTIVE MEMBERSHIPS, SAID COMMITTEE TO REPORT ITS FINDINGS TO THE TOWN NOT LATER THAN THE
NEXT ANNUAL TOWN MEETING, SAID REPORT TO INCLUDE SPECIFICRECOMMENDATIONS CONCERNING THE NEED FOR
EITHER A PROFESSIONAL ENGINEERING WATER SURVEY TO PROVIDE THE TOWN WITH A MASTER PLAN WITH TIMETABLE
AND COST ESTIMATES WHERE POSSIBLE, OR A CONTINUING WATER NEEDS STUDY INDEPENDENT OF A PROFESSIONAL
SURVEY, AND IF A PROFESSIONAL SURVEY IS RECOMMENDED THE REPORT SHALL INCLUDE THE APPROXIMATE COST OF
SUCH PROFESSIONAL SURVEY AND A SUMMARY OF ITEMS TO BE INCLUDED THEREIN.

OF THE 250TH ANNIVERSARY COMMITTEE, SUCH COMMITTEE TO BE AUTHORIZED TO ENTER INTO ANY CONTRACTS
THEREFOR.
Article 56. To see if the Townwill voteto authorize the Town Treasurer, with the approval of the Selectmen, to borrow money
from time to time in anticipation of the revenue of the financial year beginning July 1, 1975, in accordance with the provisions of
the General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 4, and to issue a note or notes therefor, payable within one year, and to renew any note or
notes as may be given for a period of less than one year in accordance with the General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 17, or act any-
thing thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 57. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Selectmen tosell at public auctionany and all property foreclosed
up to this time and which may be foreclosed during Fiscal Year 1976 under Tax Title proceedings and to sign, execute, and deliver
in the name of the Town, proper deeds of conveyance or release thereof, the said auction to beadvertised fourteen (14)days before
the sale, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 58. To see if the Town will vote to choose a Director in accordance with Chapter 128 of the General Laws. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY THAT SUE LOGAN BE CHOSEN DIRECTOR IN ACCORDANCE WITH CHAPTER 128 OF THE GENERAL LAWS.
Article 59. To see if the Town will vote to accept the several sums of money for Perpetual Care of various cemetery lots, ap-

June 2, 1975
Article 1. To see if the Town will vote to rescind the vote taken under Article 3of the Warrant forthe Special Town Meeting
held January 28, 1974, relative to the proposed relocation of Route 2A, or act anything thereon. MOTION DEFEATED.

Town Meeting under Articles 42 and 43 and Item 51 of the Budget, a sum of money for general maintenance and repair of town roads,
or act anything thereon. (Approved by Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $45,000.00
FOR GENERAL MAINTENANCE AND REPAIR OF TOWN ROADS IN FISCAL 1976 AND THAT TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION
$7,000.00 BETRANSFERRED FROMFUNDS APPROPRIATED UNDER ARTICLE 42 OF THE 1975 ANNUAL MEET ING WARRANT FOR
CHAPTER 90 IMPROVEMENT OF LEOMINSTER ROAD, $12,800.00 BE TRANSFERRED FROMFUNDS APPROPRIATED UNDER ARTICLE
43 OF THE 1975 ANNUAL MEETING WARRANT FOR COUNTY SHARES OF CHAPTER HIGHWAY WORK, $11,572.00 BE TRANS-
FERRED FROM FUNDS APPROPRIATED UNDER ARTICLE 5 OF THE 1975 ANNUAL MEETING WARRANT AS ITEM 51 CHAPTER 90
IMPROVEMENT AND $13,628.00 BE TRANSFERRED FROM FUNDS APPROPRIATED UNDER ARTICLE 42 OF THE 1974 ANNUAL
MEETING WARRANT FOR HIGHWAY CONSTRUCTION, MAINTENANCE AND REPAIR.
Article 3. To see If the Town will vote to appropriate from available funds, the sumof $16,162.00, or some other sumor sums,
for bridge repairs and/or general highway construction, maintenance and repairs, or act anything thereon. VOTED TO APPROPRIATE
FROM FUNDS RECEIVED FROM THE STATE IN ACCORDANCE WITH SECTION 1, CHAPTER 825, ACTS OF 1974, THE SUM OF
$16,162.00 FOR BRIDGE REPAIRS AND/OR GENERAL HIGHWAY CONSTRUCTION, MAINTENANCE AND REPAIRS COMMENC-
ING IN FISCAL 1976.
Article 4. To see if the Town will vote to ratifyand confirm the conveyance of a tract of land on Main Street in the center of
Lunenburg, Massachusetts, by deed of the Board of Selectmen purportedly acting in behalf of the Town, to Warren Lewis, by deed
dated June 12, 1911, recorded with Worcester Northern District Deeds, Book 270, Page 301, or act anything thereon. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 4.
